```sh
cd /Users/sethml/src/brepper && git commit -F - <<'EOF'
Fix spurious sphere hypothesis on small cubes (1mm manual cube)

Problem: the 1mm manual cube got a spurious sphere hypothesis because
all 8 vertices of a cube lie exactly on the circumscribed sphere
(r=√3/2). The centroid validation used a fixed surface_tolerance
(0.4mm), which was too generous for 1mm models — the centroid-to-sphere
error of 0.366mm was just below the threshold.

Root cause analysis:
- For a cube of side s, face centroids are at distance s√2/2 from
  center, while the sphere radius is s√3/2. The centroid error is
  s(√3-√2)/2 ≈ 0.159s (per edge) and 0.366s (per centroid).
- For s=10mm: 3.66mm > 0.4mm → rejected by centroid check ✓
- For s=1mm: 0.366mm < 0.4mm → passes centroid check ✗
- Normal alignment doesn't help: cube face normals are exactly radial
  from center (cos angle = 1.0) because face centroids lie on principal
  axes through the cube center.

Fix: add a sagitta-based centroid check alongside the existing
surface_tolerance check. For each face, compute the expected sagitta
as R - sqrt(R² - (h/2)²) where h is the longest edge. If the actual
centroid-to-sphere distance exceeds 2× expected sagitta + vertex_tol,
reject the hypothesis. For the 1mm cube: expected sagitta from edge
length 1.0 is 0.159mm, so tolerance = 0.318mm + 1e-5 ≈ 0.318mm,
which rejects the actual centroid distance of 0.366mm.

Why the tests didn't catch this:
1. cube_produces_six_planar_hypotheses only checked planar counts,
   not that spherical_hypotheses.len() == 0
2. chamfered_cube_produces_no_spherical_hypotheses used the 10mm
   Onshape model, not the 1mm manual cube
3. manual_cube_stage2_compare ran at stage 2.1, not 2.3
4. No manual_cube_stage23_compare test existed

Added tests:
- manual_cube_produces_no_spherical_hypotheses (count test)
- manual_cube_stage23_compare (compare test)

186 tests pass, zero clippy warnings.

Note: the 1mm cube still gets a spurious cylinder from stage 2.2
(r=1/√2, all 4 lateral vertices lie on a cylinder). Same root cause,
separate fix needed.
```

- ⬜ Commit changes

You're right. The sagitta-based centroid validation was misguided. A cube **is** a sphere rendered at low resolution — the tessellation of a sphere at very coarse resolution produces flat faces whose vertices all lie exactly on the sphere. A cube is the limiting case (the minimum-face polyhedron inscribed in a sphere with all faces identical). The centroid deviation from the sphere surface is exactly what you'd expect from coarse tessellation, not evidence that it's not a sphere. The "fix" was essentially saying "this sphere is too coarsely tessellated to be a real sphere" — which is an arbitrary aesthetic judgment, not a geometric one. The real answer is: the compare test should verify that every STEP surface has a matching hypothesis of the correct type.

## 2. Compare Logic in src/lib.rs and src/stage1.rs

There's **no `run_pipeline_for_compare()` or `CompareResult`** — instead, the compare flow uses **error structures**:

**Stage 1 Compare Error:** [src/stage1.rs](src/stage1.rs#L698-L809)

```rust
#[derive(Debug)]
pub struct CompareError {
    pub vertex_max_distance: f64,
    pub vertex_max_index: usize,
    pub vertex_tolerance: f64,
    pub centroid_max_distance: f64,
    pub centroid_max_face_index: usize,
    pub centroid_tolerance: f64,
}

impl Display for CompareError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        let mut parts = Vec::new();
        if self.vertex_max_distance > self.vertex_tolerance {
            parts.push(format!(
                "vertex {} is {:.6e} mm from nearest STEP surface (tolerance: {:.6e} mm)",
                self.vertex_max_index, self.vertex_max_distance, self.vertex_tolerance
            ));
        }
        if self.centroid_max_distance > self.centroid_tolerance {
            parts.push(format!(
                "face {} centroid is {:.6e} mm from nearest STEP surface (tolerance: {:.6e} mm)",
                self.centroid_max_face_index, self.centroid_max_distance, self.centroid_tolerance
            ));
        }
        write!(f, "comparison failed: {}", parts.join("; "))
    }
}
```

**Stage 2 Compare Error:** [src/stage2.rs](src/stage2.rs#L120-L144)

```rust
#[derive(Debug)]
pub struct Stage2CompareError {
    pub hypothesis_type: &'static str,
    pub hypothesis_index: usize,
    pub max_distance: f64,
    pub tolerance: f64,
}

impl Display for Stage2CompareError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        write!(
            f,
            "{} hypothesis {} projected centroid is {:.6e} mm from nearest STEP surface (tolerance: {:.6e} mm)",
            self.hypothesis_type, self.hypothesis_index, self.max_distance, self.tolerance
        )
    }
}
```

**Core comparison function:** [src/stage1.rs](src/stage1.rs#L774-826)

```rust
pub fn min_distance_to_shape(
    pt: &gp::Pnt,
    compare_shape: &opencascade_sys::topo_ds::Shape,
) -> f64 {
    let mut vertex = b_rep_builder_api::MakeVertex::new_pnt(pt);
    let vtx_shape = vertex.vertex();
    let progress = message::ProgressRange::new();
    let dist_calc = b_rep_extrema::DistShapeShape::new_shape2_extflag_extalgo_progressrange(
        vtx_shape.as_shape(),
        compare_shape,
        0, // F (unused/obsolete)
        extrema::ExtAlgo::Grad,
        &progress,
    );
    if dist_calc.is_done() && dist_calc.nb_solution() > 0 {
        dist_calc.value()
    } else {
        f64::MAX
    }
}

fn compare_mesh_to_step(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, config: &Config) -> Result<(), CompareError> {
    let compare_shape = config.compare_shape.as_ref().unwrap();

    // Check all vertices
    let mut vtx_max_dist = 0.0_f64;
    let mut vtx_max_idx = 0_usize;
    for (i, v) in mesh.vertices.iter().enumerate() {
        let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(v.x, v.y, v.z);
        let d = min_distance_to_shape(&pt, compare_shape);
        if d > vtx_max_dist {
            vtx_max_dist = d;
            vtx_max_idx = i;
        }
    }

    // Check all face centroids
    let mut ctr_max_dist = 0.0_f64;
    let mut ctr_max_face = 0_usize;
    for (fi, face) in mesh.faces.iter().enumerate() {
        let n = face.vertex_count as usize;
        let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
        let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
        let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
        for vi in 0..n {
            let v = &mesh.vertices[face.vertex_indices[vi]];
            cx += v.x;
            cy += v.y;
            cz += v.z;
        }
        let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
        let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(cx * inv_n, cy * inv_n, cz * inv_n);
        let d = min_distance_to_shape(&pt, compare_shape);
        if d > ctr_max_dist {
            ctr_max_dist = d;
            ctr_max_face = fi;
        }
    }

    if !config.quiet {
        eprintln!(
            "  Compare: vertex max dist {:.6e} mm, centroid max dist {:.6e} mm",
            vtx_max_dist, ctr_max_dist
        );
    }

    let vtx_fail = vtx_max_dist > config.vertex_tolerance_mm;
    let ctr_fail = ctr_max_dist > config.surface_tolerance_mm;

    if vtx_fail || ctr_fail {
        return Err(CompareError {
            vertex_max_distance: vtx_max_dist,
            vertex_max_index: vtx_max_idx,
            vertex_tolerance: config.vertex_tolerance_mm,
            centroid_max_distance: ctr_max_dist,
            centroid_max_face_index: ctr_max_face,
            centroid_tolerance: config.surface_tolerance_mm,
        });
    }

    Ok(())
}
```

**How comparison works:**
- Uses OCCT's `BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape` with `ExtAlgo::Grad` to find bounded distance from points to the compare STEP shape
- Projections respect face trimming boundaries
- Checks: (1) all mesh vertices, (2) all face centroids against STEP shape

## 3. Hypothesis Structures (Stage 2) [src/stage2.rs](src/stage2.rs#L23-91)

```rust
/// A planar surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct PlanarHypothesis {
    /// Unit normal vector pointing outward from the shell/solid.
    pub normal: [f64; 3],
    /// Signed distance from origin to plane along the normal.
    pub distance: f64,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this plane.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum (positive) distance from any vertex to the plane.
    pub error_max: f64,
    /// Minimum (most negative) distance from any vertex to the plane.
    pub error_min: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-plane distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// A cylindrical surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct CylindricalHypothesis {
    /// A point on the cylinder axis.
    pub axis_origin: [f64; 3],
    /// Unit direction vector along the cylinder axis.
    pub axis_direction: [f64; 3],
    /// Radius of the cylinder (always positive).
    pub radius: f64,
    /// Whether the surface normal points away from the axis (convex=true) or toward it (concave=false).
    pub convex: bool,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this cylinder.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum absolute distance from any vertex to the cylinder surface.
    pub error_max: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-surface distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// A spherical surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct SphericalHypothesis {
    /// Center of the sphere.
    pub center: [f64; 3],
    /// Radius of the sphere (always positive).
    pub radius: f64,
    /// Whether the surface normal points away from center (convex) or toward it (concave).
    pub convex: bool,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this sphere.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum absolute distance from any vertex to the sphere surface.
    pub error_max: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-surface distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// Identifies a selected surface hypothesis by type and index.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy)]
pub enum SelectedSurface {
    Planar(usize),
    Cylindrical(usize),
    Spherical(usize),
}
```

```rust
/// The output of Stage 2: the mesh with all hypotheses populated and surfaces selected.
#[derive(Debug)]
pub struct Stage2Output {
    /// The mesh from stage 1 with per-face hypothesis indices populated.
    pub mesh: ConnectedMesh,
    /// All planar hypotheses deduced in stage 2.1.
    pub planar_hypotheses: Vec<PlanarHypothesis>,
    /// All cylindrical hypotheses deduced in stage 2.2.
    pub cylindrical_hypotheses: Vec<CylindricalHypothesis>,
    /// All spherical hypotheses deduced in stage 2.3.
    pub spherical_hypotheses: Vec<SphericalHypothesis>,
    /// Surfaces selected in stage 2.6 for reconstruction.
    pub selected_surfaces: Vec<SelectedSurface>,
}
```

## 4. How --compare is Invoked [src/bin/brepper.rs](src/bin/brepper.rs#L36-40)

```rust
fn main() {
    let mut config = config::parse_config();

    if let Err(e) = config.load_compare_step() {
        eprintln!("Error loading comparison STEP file: {e}");
        process::exit(1);
    }

    if let Err(e) = run(&config) {
        eprintln!("Error: {e}");
        process::exit(1);
    }
}
```

The pipeline flow:
1. Parse CLI (including `--compare` flag)
2. **Load STEP shape** via `config.load_compare_step()` [src/config.rs](src/config.rs#L240-276)
3. Run stage 1, stage 2, etc. — **they all check if** `config.compare_shape.is_some()`
4. Compare checks happen **automatically at each stage**

**Where STEP surfaces are loaded:** [src/config.rs](src/config.rs#L240-276)

```rust
pub fn load_compare_step(&mut self) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let step_path = match &self.compare_step {
        Some(p) => p.clone(),
        None => return Ok(()),
    };

    let mut reader = step_control::Reader::new();
    reader.read_file_charptr(&step_path);
    let progress = message::ProgressRange::new();
    reader.transfer_roots(&progress);
    let step_shape = reader.one_shape();

    // Count faces for validation and status output
    let mut face_count = 0_usize;
    let mut explorer = top_exp::Explorer::new_shape_shapeenum2(
        &step_shape,
        top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face,
        top_abs::ShapeEnum::Shape,
    );
    while explorer.more() {
        face_count += 1;
        explorer.next();
    }

    if face_count == 0 {
        return Err(format!("STEP file {step_path} contains no faces").into());
    }

    if !self.quiet {
        eprintln!("Loaded {} faces from comparison STEP file", face_count);
    }

    self.compare_face_count = face_count;
    self.compare_shape = Some(step_shape);

    Ok(())
}
```

**Config fields storing comparison shape:** [src/config.rs](src/config.rs#L181-186)

```rust
/// STEP file path to compare against at each stage.
pub compare_step: Option<String>,
/// Shape loaded from the --compare STEP file, for bounded distance checks.
pub compare_shape: Option<opencascade_sys::OwnedPtr<opencascade_sys::topo_ds::Shape>>,
/// Number of faces in the compare shape (for status output).
pub compare_face_count: usize,
```

**Type information available in STEP:** The OCCT shape contains **face topologies** (via `top_exp::Explorer`), but the current code doesn't extract surface type info (plane/cylinder/sphere). It treats the STEP shape as a black box for bounded distance calculation.

```
// Stage 2.1: Compare check
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Validate fitted planar hypotheses against a reference STEP shape.
///
/// For each hypothesis, projects face centroids onto the fitted plane and
/// checks that those projected points are within surface_tolerance of the
/// reference STEP surface.
fn compare_planar_hypotheses(
    hypotheses: &[PlanarHypothesis],
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    config: &Config,
) -> Result<(), Stage2CompareError> {
    let compare_shape = config.compare_shape.as_ref().unwrap();

    for (hi, hyp) in hypotheses.iter().enumerate() {
        let mut max_dist = 0.0_f64;

        for &fi in &hyp.faces {
            let face = &mesh.faces[fi];
            let n = face.vertex_count as usize;
            let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
            let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
            let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
            for vi_idx in 0..n {
                let v = &mesh.vertices[face.vertex_indices[vi_idx]];
                cx += v.x;
                cy += v.y;
                cz += v.z;
            }
            let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
            cx *= inv_n;
            cy *= inv_n;
            cz *= inv_n;

            // Project centroid onto the fitted plane
            let dist_to_plane = hyp.normal[0] * cx + hyp.normal[1] * cy + hyp.normal[2] * cz
                - hyp.distance;
            let px = cx - dist_to_plane * hyp.normal[0];
            let py = cy - dist_to_plane * hyp.normal[1];
            let pz = cz - dist_to_plane * hyp.normal[2];

            let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(px, py, pz);
            let d = stage1::min_distance_to_shape(&pt, compare_shape);
            max_dist = max_dist.max(d);
        }

        if max_dist > config.surface_tolerance_mm {
            return Err(Stage2CompareError {
                hypothesis_type: "planar",
                hypothesis_index: hi,
                max_distance: max_dist,
                tolerance: config.surface_tolerance_mm,
            });
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stage 2.2: Cylindrical hypothesis deduction
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

const MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD: f64 = 0.01;

/// Compute the face centroid for a mesh face.
fn face_centroid(face: &MeshFace, vertices: &[MeshVertex]) -> [f64; 3] {
    let n = face.vertex_count as usize;
    let mut cx = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cy = 0.0_f64;
    let mut cz = 0.0_f64;
    for vi_idx in 0..n {
        let v = &vertices[face.vertex_indices[vi_idx]];
        cx += v.x;
        cy += v.y;
        cz += v.z;
    }
    let inv_n = 1.0 / n as f64;
    [cx * inv_n, cy * inv_n, cz * inv_n]
```
</details>

Based on my comprehensive exploration of the opencascade-rs bindings, here are the Rust FFI bindings for extracting surface types from a TopoDS_Face:

## **Key Binding Files**

| File | Location | Purpose |
|------|----------|---------|
| [geom_abs.rs](geom_abs.rs) | `/opencascade-sys/generated/geom_abs.rs` | Enum definitions |
| [b_rep.rs](b_rep.rs) | `/opencascade-sys/generated/b_rep.rs` | Stool methods |

## **Surface Type Enum**

**File:** [geom_abs.rs](geom_abs.rs#L170-L190)

```rust
/// C++ enum: `GeomAbs_SurfaceType`
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, Hash)]
#[repr(i32)]
pub enum SurfaceType {
    Plane = 0,
    Cylinder = 1,
    Cone = 2,
    Sphere = 3,
    Torus = 4,
    Beziersurface = 5,
    Bsplinesurface = 6,
    Surfaceofrevolution = 7,
    Surfaceofextrusion = 8,
    Offsetsurface = 9,
    Othersurface = 10,
}
```

Used as: `geom_abs::SurfaceType`

## **Extract Surface from Face**

**File:** [b_rep.rs](b_rep.rs#L10129-L10137)

```rust
pub fn surface_face(
    F: &crate::topo_ds::Face,
) -> crate::OwnedPtr<crate::ffi_types::HandleGeomSurface> {
    unsafe {
        crate::OwnedPtr::from_raw(crate::check_result(
            crate::ffi_extern_TKBRep::BRep_Tool_surface_face(F),
        ))
    }
}
```

**Usage:**
```rust
use opencascade_sys::{b_rep, geom_abs};

let surface_handle = b_rep::Tool::surface_face(&face);
// surface_handle is OwnedPtr<HandleGeomSurface>
```

## **Get Surface Type - Two Approaches**

Based on the C++ API, you have two main options (though complete Rust bindings for GetType() on Geom_Surface may need to be added):

**Approach 1:** Create a **GeomAdaptor_Surface** from the surface handle, then call `GetType()`:
```cpp
// C++ pattern:
GeomAdaptor_Surface adaptor(surf);
GeomAbs_SurfaceType type = adaptor.GetType();
```

**Approach 2:** Use dynamic type information (RTTI) if the surface is a concrete type:
```cpp
// C++ pattern - dynamic casting:
if (Handle(Geom_Cylinder)(*surf)) { /* it's a cylinder */ }
if (Handle(Geom_Plane)(*surf)) { /* it's a plane */ }
// etc.
```

## **Naming Conventions** (from PORTING.md)

- `GeomAdaptor_Surface` → `geom_adaptor::Surface` (module name is snake_case prefix, type is suffix)
- `GeomAbs_SurfaceType` → `geom_abs::SurfaceType`
- `BRep_Tool` → `b_rep::Tool`
- Constructor: `::new_handlegeom_surface()` for Handle argument

## **Complete Example Pattern**

```rust
use opencascade_sys::{b_rep, geom_abs, geom_adaptor};

// 1. Extract surface from face
let surface_handle = b_rep::Tool::surface_face(&face);

// 2. Create adaptor (would need GeomAdaptor_Surface bindings)
// let adaptor = geom_adaptor::Surface::new_handlegeom_surface(&surface_handle);
// let surf_type = adaptor.get_type(); // Returns geom_abs::SurfaceType
```

**Note:** The bindings for `GeomAdaptor_Surface::GetType()` should be available as `geom_adaptor::Surface::get_type()` once fully exposed. You may need to verify the exact method signature in the generated `geom_adaptor.rs` module, as some advanced surface adaptor methods might require accessing through the adaptor interface.

</details>

`BRepAdaptor_Surface::get_type()` returns `geom_abs::SurfaceType`. Now let me see how to construct it from a `TopoDS_Face`:

Read [b_rep_adaptor.rs](/Users/sethml/src/opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-sys/generated/b_rep_adaptor.rs)

Now I have the full picture. The approach is:

1. Iterate STEP faces with `TopExp_Explorer`
2. For each face, create a  and call  to get `GeomAbs_SurfaceType`
3. Map to our hypothesis types (Plane→Planar, Cylinder→Cylindrical, Sphere→Spherical)
4. For each STEP surface, check that there's at least one hypothesis of the matching type whose projected centroids are within tolerance of that STEP face

Now let me check how `TopExp_Explorer` is used to get individual faces (not just the whole shape):

Searched for regex `Explorer.*Face|explorer.*face.*current\|explorer.*next` (`**/src/**/*.rs`), no results

```
//! Integration tests for Stage 2: surface fitting.
//!
//! Tests that planar hypothesis deduction produces correct results for
//! known planar models, and that --compare validation passes.

use brepper::config;
use brepper::{stage1, stage2};

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Helpers
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

fn manifest_dir() -> String {
    env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR").to_string()
}

fn config_for_stl(stl_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--stage=2.1", "-q"]).unwrap()
}

fn config_for_compare(stl_path: &str, step_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    let mut config =
        config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--compare", step_path, "--stage=2.1", "-q"])
            .unwrap();
    config.load_compare_step().unwrap();
    config
}
fn config_for_stl_stage22(stl_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--stage=2.2", "-q"]).unwrap()
}

fn config_for_compare_stage22(stl_path: &str, step_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    let mut config =
        config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--compare", step_path, "--stage=2.2", "-q"])
            .unwrap();
    config.load_compare_step().unwrap();
    config
}

/// Run stages 1 and 2.1, returning the Stage2Output.
fn run_stage2(config: &config::Config) -> stage2::Stage2Output {
    let mesh = stage1::stage1(config).expect("stage1 should pass");
    stage2::stage2(config, mesh).expect("stage2 should pass")
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Macros for generating test cases
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Generate a test that runs stage 2.1 with --compare against a STEP file.
macro_rules! test_stl_step_stage2_compare {
    ($name:ident, $stl_path:literal, $step_path:literal) => {
        #[test]
        fn $name() {
            let stl = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $stl_path);
            let step = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $step_path);
            let config = config_for_compare(&stl, &step);
            run_stage2(&config);
        }
    };
}

/// Generate a test that runs stage 2.2 with --compare against a STEP file.
macro_rules! test_stl_step_stage22_compare {
    ($name:ident, $stl_path:literal, $step_path:literal) => {
        #[test]
        fn $name() {
            let stl = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $stl_path);
            let step = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $step_path);
            let config = config_for_compare_stage22(&stl, &step);
            run_stage2(&config);
        }
    };
}

// ===========================================================================
// Planar hypothesis count tests
// ===========================================================================

#[test]
fn cube_produces_six_planar_hypotheses() {
    let stl = format!("{}/tests/manual/cube.stl", manifest_dir());
    let config = config_for_stl(&stl);
    let output = run_stage2(&config);

    assert_eq!(
        output.planar_hypotheses.len(),
        6,
        "cube should have 6 planar hypotheses"
    );
    for h in &output.planar_hypotheses {
        assert_eq!(h.faces.len(), 1, "each cube face hypothesis should have 1 quad");
    }
}

#[test]
fn wedge_produces_six_planar_hypotheses() {
    let stl = format!("{}/tests/ccad/generated/wedge.stl", manifest_dir());
    let config = config_for_stl(&stl);
    let output = run_stage2(&config);
```

```
//! Integration tests for Stage 2: surface fitting.
//!
//! Tests that planar hypothesis deduction produces correct results for
//! known planar models, and that --compare validation passes.

use brepper::config;
use brepper::{stage1, stage2};

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Helpers
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

fn manifest_dir() -> String {
    env!("CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR").to_string()
}

fn config_for_stl(stl_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--stage=2.1", "-q"]).unwrap()
}

fn config_for_compare(stl_path: &str, step_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    let mut config =
        config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--compare", step_path, "--stage=2.1", "-q"])
            .unwrap();
    config.load_compare_step().unwrap();
    config
}
fn config_for_stl_stage22(stl_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--stage=2.2", "-q"]).unwrap()
}

fn config_for_compare_stage22(stl_path: &str, step_path: &str) -> config::Config {
    let mut config =
        config::parse_config_from(["brepper", stl_path, "--compare", step_path, "--stage=2.2", "-q"])
            .unwrap();
    config.load_compare_step().unwrap();
    config
}

/// Run stages 1 and 2.1, returning the Stage2Output.
fn run_stage2(config: &config::Config) -> stage2::Stage2Output {
    let mesh = stage1::stage1(config).expect("stage1 should pass");
    stage2::stage2(config, mesh).expect("stage2 should pass")
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Macros for generating test cases
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Generate a test that runs stage 2.1 with --compare against a STEP file.
macro_rules! test_stl_step_stage2_compare {
    ($name:ident, $stl_path:literal, $step_path:literal) => {
        #[test]
        fn $name() {
            let stl = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $stl_path);
            let step = format!("{}/{}", manifest_dir(), $step_path);
            let config = config_for_compare(&stl, &step);
            run_stage2(&config);
        }
    };
}

macro_rules! test_stl_step_stage22_compare {
```

Let me write the helper function. I need to compute face centroids, project them onto hypothesis surfaces, and then check distance to individual STEP faces.

Actually wait — I should re-read what projection I need. For the "surface type coverage" check, I don't actually need to project onto the hypothesis surface. I just need to check that a hypothesis of the matching type has face centroids that are *close to the STEP face*. The face centroids in the mesh lie on the STL mesh, which is an approximation of the STEP geometry. So just checking raw centroid distance to the STEP face (without projection onto the hypothesis) should work for confirming coverage.

Actually, let me think about this more carefully. The centroid of a mesh face that lies on a cylindrical surface will be slightly inside the cylinder (due to the chord-to-arc gap). But it should still be within `surface_tolerance` of the STEP face. Similarly for spheres.

So the logic is:
1. For each STEP face, get its surface type
2. For each hypothesis of matching type, compute centroids of its mesh faces
3. If any centroid of any matching hypothesis is within `surface_tolerance` of the STEP face, mark that STEP face as covered
4. Assert all STEP faces (of known types: Plane, Cylinder, Sphere) are covered

```
}
```

```sh
Add surface-type coverage checks to stage 2.2 and 2.3 compare tests

Enhanced compare test macros for stages 2.2 and 2.3 to verify that

hypothesis of the matching type. This catches the case where a model

  matching hypothesis (Plane→PlanarHypothesis, Cylinder→Cylindrical,
  Sphere→Spherical) with at least one mesh face centroid within
  surface_tolerance of the STEP face.
- Stage 2.2 macro checks Plane and Cylinder types (spheres not yet
  deduced at this stage).
- Stage 2.3 macro checks Plane, Cylinder, and Sphere types.

  tests — both pass because the cube's 6 planar STEP faces are all
  covered by planar hypotheses, while any spurious cylinder/sphere
  hypotheses are irrelevant (no STEP face has those types).
```

Done. Here's the summary:

**Why the sagitta fix was wrong:** You're right — a cube *is* a sphere rendered at minimum resolution. All 8 vertices lie exactly on the circumscribed sphere, so the vertex fitting error is exactly zero. The "sagitta-based centroid validation" was an arbitrary heuristic that said "this sphere looks too coarsely tessellated" — but that's not a valid geometric objection. The fix was rejecting a geometrically valid interpretation.

**What I did instead:**

1. **Reverted the sagitta fix** (soft-reset + checkout).

2. **Enhanced compare tests** with  — for every STEP face, it uses  to determine the surface type (Plane/Cylinder/Sphere), then verifies there's at least one hypothesis of the *matching* type with a mesh face centroid within tolerance of that STEP face.

3. **Stage 2.2 tests** check Plane and Cylinder coverage (spheres not deduced yet).  
   **Stage 2.3 tests** check Plane, Cylinder, and Sphere coverage.

4. **Added ** — both pass because the cube's 6 planar STEP faces are covered by 6 planar hypotheses. The spurious cylinder/sphere hypotheses are harmless since no STEP face has those types.

```
use clap::{Parser, ValueEnum};
use opencascade_sys::{message, step_control, top_abs, top_exp};
use std::fmt::{Display, Formatter};

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stage
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Stage identifier as (major, minor). Minor=0 means run all sub-stages of that major stage.
/// Examples: (1, 2) = stage 1.2, (2, 0) = all of stage 2, (4, 1) = full pipeline.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
pub struct Stage(pub u8, pub u8);

impl Stage {
    /// The final sub-stage for each major stage.
    fn last_minor(major: u8) -> u8 {
        match major {
            1 => 3,
            2 => 6,
            3 => 6,
            4 => 1,
            _ => 0,
        }
    }

    /// Normalize: if minor is 0, expand to last sub-stage of that major.
    fn normalized(self) -> (u8, u8) {
        if self.1 == 0 {
            (self.0, Self::last_minor(self.0))
        } else {
            (self.0, self.1)
        }
    }

    /// Returns true if the pipeline should run at least through the given stage.
    pub fn at_least(&self, major: u8, minor: u8) -> bool {
        let (sm, ss) = self.normalized();
        (sm, ss) >= (major, minor)
    }
}

impl Default for Stage {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Stage(4, 1)
    }
}

fn parse_stage(s: &str) -> Result<Stage, String> {
    if let Some((major_s, minor_s)) = s.split_once('.') {
        let major: u8 = major_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        let minor: u8 = minor_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) || !(1..=Stage::last_minor(major)).contains(&minor) {
            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1.1–4.1)"));
        }
        Ok(Stage(major, minor))
    } else {
        let major: u8 = s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) {
            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1–4)"));
        }
        Ok(Stage(major, 0))
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Units
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Length units for STL/STEP files.
#[derive(Debug, Default, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, ValueEnum)]
pub enum Units {
    /// Millimeters
    #[default]
    Mm,
    /// Centimeters
    Cm,
    /// Meters
    M,
    /// Inches
    In,
    /// Feet
    Ft,
    /// Micrometers
    Um,
}

impl Units {
    /// Conversion factor: 1 of this unit = N millimeters.
    pub fn to_mm(self) -> f64 {
        match self {
            Units::Mm => 1.0,
            Units::Cm => 10.0,
            Units::M => 1000.0,
            Units::In => 25.4,
            Units::Ft => 304.8,
            Units::Um => 0.001,
        }
    }
}


impl Display for Units {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        match self {
            Units::Mm => write!(f, "mm"),
            Units::Cm => write!(f, "cm"),
            Units::M => write!(f, "m"),
            Units::In => write!(f, "in"),
            Units::Ft => write!(f, "ft"),
            Units::Um => write!(f, "μm"),
        }
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// CLI args (parsed by clap)
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

```
use clap::{Parser, ValueEnum};
use opencascade_sys::{message, step_control, top_abs, top_exp};
use std::fmt::{Display, Formatter};

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stage
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Stage identifier as (major, minor). Minor=0 means run all sub-stages of that major stage.
/// Examples: (1, 2) = stage 1.2, (2, 0) = all of stage 2, (4, 1) = full pipeline.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
pub struct Stage(pub u8, pub u8);

impl Stage {
    /// The final sub-stage for each major stage.
    fn last_minor(major: u8) -> u8 {
        match major {
            1 => 3,
            2 => 6,
            3 => 6,
            4 => 1,
            _ => 0,
        }
    }

    /// Normalize: if minor is 0, expand to last sub-stage of that major.
    fn normalized(self) -> (u8, u8) {
        if self.1 == 0 {
            (self.0, Self::last_minor(self.0))
        } else {
            (self.0, self.1)
        }
    }

    /// Returns true if the pipeline should run at least through the given stage.
    pub fn at_least(&self, major: u8, minor: u8) -> bool {
        let (sm, ss) = self.normalized();
        (sm, ss) >= (major, minor)
    }
}

impl Default for Stage {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Stage(4, 1)
    }
}

fn parse_stage(s: &str) -> Result<Stage, String> {
    if let Some((major_s, minor_s)) = s.split_once('.') {
        let major: u8 = major_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        let minor: u8 = minor_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) || !(1..=Stage::last_minor(major)).contains(&minor) {
            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1.1–4.1)"));
        }
        Ok(Stage(major, minor))
    } else {
        let major: u8 = s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) {
            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1–4)"));
        }
        Ok(Stage(major, 0))
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Units
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Length units for STL/STEP files.
#[derive(Debug, Default, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, ValueEnum)]
pub enum Units {
    /// Millimeters
    #[default]
    Mm,
    /// Centimeters
    Cm,
    /// Meters
    M,
    /// Inches
    In,
    /// Feet
    Ft,
    /// Micrometers
    Um,
}

impl Units {
    /// Conversion factor: 1 of this unit = N millimeters.
    pub fn to_mm(self) -> f64 {
        match self {
            Units::Mm => 1.0,
            Units::Cm => 10.0,
            Units::M => 1000.0,
            Units::In => 25.4,
            Units::Ft => 304.8,
            Units::Um => 0.001,
        }
    }
}


impl Display for Units {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        match self {
            Units::Mm => write!(f, "mm"),
            Units::Cm => write!(f, "cm"),
            Units::M => write!(f, "m"),
            Units::In => write!(f, "in"),
            Units::Ft => write!(f, "ft"),
            Units::Um => write!(f, "μm"),
        }
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// CLI args (parsed by clap)
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

Now let me understand the concept. The angular tolerance is the maximum dihedral angle between adjacent mesh triangles on the same surface. For a cube (a purely planar surface), adjacent faces have 90° dihedral angles — far exceeding any reasonable angular tolerance (17.5° default). This means a pair of adjacent faces on the cube should NOT be considered as lying on the same curved surface.

The check should go in the BFS **seeding** step: when we look for a seed pair of adjacent faces, the current code only checks that  (0.01 ≈ 0.6°). But the angle between adjacent faces on a cube is 90°. The angular tolerance check should reject seed pairs where the dihedral angle exceeds the angular tolerance.

Now I have a clear picture. The angular tolerance is the maximum dihedral angle between adjacent triangles that can belong to the same surface. For a cube, the dihedral angle between adjacent faces is 90°. With the default of 17.5°, seed pairs on the cube would be rejected.

The key insight: the angular tolerance check should be applied during **BFS expansion** (not just seeding), because we need to check that every face being added to a hypothesis has a dihedral angle ≤ angular_tolerance with its BFS-parent face. However, for the **seed**, the check is most critical too — the seed pair's dihedral angle must be ≤ angular_tolerance.

Let me also check: the cross product magnitude equals sin(angle), not the angle itself. For the angular tolerance, I need to compute the actual angle between normals.

Let me also check: what is the EXACT dihedral angle between neighboring faces of the cube? The cube's faces meet at 90°, and the STL normal is the outward face normal. The angle between two adjacent face normals on a cube is exactly 90° (since they are orthogonal planes). So 17.5° (π/8 ≈ 0.305 rad) would definitely reject them.

**Todo list:**
- ✅ Read LEARNINGS.md, DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md
- ✅ Understand angular tolerance concept

Now I need to add the `angular_tol` parameter to both functions and add the angular check. The key concept: two adjacent faces on the same curved surface (cylinder/sphere) should have normals that differ by at most the angular tolerance. On a cube, adjacent face normals differ by 90° — far exceeding 17.5°.

The check should be applied:
1. **At seed pair creation**: the dihedral angle between seed faces must be ≤ angular_tolerance  
2. **During BFS expansion**: the dihedral angle between the current face and each neighbor must be ≤ angular_tolerance

For (2), I need to think carefully. The current BFS pops a face from the queue and checks its neighbors. The dihedral angle should be between the candidate neighbor and its BFS-parent (the current face). This naturally limits how fast the surface can curve.

The dihedral angle between two faces with normals n1, n2 is `acos(dot(n1, n2))`. But we need to be careful: for adjacent faces on a convex cylinder, the normals point away from each other, so the dot product is positive but < 1. The angle between normals IS the dihedral angle.

Let me now edit the cylindrical deduction to add the angular tolerance parameter and check:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 796-870 of <a href="../src/stage2.rs#L796-L871">stage2.rs</a></summary>

```
fn deduce_cylindrical_hypotheses(
    mesh: &mut ConnectedMesh,
    planar_hypotheses: &[PlanarHypothesis],
    vertex_tol: f64,
    surface_tol: f64,
) -> Vec<CylindricalHypothesis> {
    let num_faces = mesh.faces.len();
    let mut hypotheses: Vec<CylindricalHypothesis> = Vec::new();

    // Initialize cylindrical_hypothesis for all faces
    for fi in 0..num_faces {
        if mesh.faces[fi].cylindrical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CYLINDRICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
            continue;
        }
        // Faces belonging to multi-face planar hypotheses are genuinely flat
        let ph = mesh.faces[fi].planar_hypothesis;
        if ph >= 0 && planar_hypotheses[ph as usize].faces.len() > 1 {
            mesh.faces[fi].cylindrical_hypothesis = NO_HYPOTHESIS;
        }
    }

    for fi in 0..num_faces {
        if mesh.faces[fi].cylindrical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CYLINDRICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
            continue;
        }

        let fi_normal = match mesh.faces[fi].normal {
            Some(n) => n,
            None => {
                mesh.faces[fi].cylindrical_hypothesis = NO_HYPOTHESIS;
                continue;
            }
        };

        // Search for a seed partner: adjacent face with sufficiently different normal
        let fi_vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        let fi_neighbors = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors;
        let mut seed_found = false;

        for &ni in &fi_neighbors[..fi_vc] {
            if ni < 0 {
                continue;
            }
            let ni = ni as usize;

            if mesh.faces[ni].cylindrical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CYLINDRICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
                continue;
            }

            // Must be a single-face planar hypothesis
            let ni_ph = mesh.faces[ni].planar_hypothesis;
            if ni_ph >= 0 && planar_hypotheses[ni_ph as usize].faces.len() > 1 {
                continue;
            }

            let ni_normal = match mesh.faces[ni].normal {
                Some(n) => n,
                None => continue,
            };

            // Check cross product magnitude for sufficient angular difference
            let cross = cross3(&fi_normal, &ni_normal);
            let cross_mag = (cross[0] * cross[0] + cross[1] * cross[1] + cross[2] * cross[2]).sqrt();
            if cross_mag < MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD {
                continue;
            }

            // Estimate initial cylinder from seed pair
            let mut axis_dir = cross;
            normalize3(&mut axis_dir);

            // Collect vertices of both seed faces
            let mut vertex_set: HashSet<usize> = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_indices[..fi_vc]
                .iter().copied().collect();
            let ni_vc = mesh.faces[ni].vertex_count as usize;
```

I need to update the seeding and BFS descriptions in both 2.2 and 2.3 to mention the angular tolerance check. Let me find the relevant parts:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 225-232 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L225-L233">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
**Seeding:**
- Initialize all `cylindrical_hypothesis` to `UNDEDUCED` (-2).
- For each face fi where `cylindrical_hypothesis == -2`:
    - If fi belongs to a multi-face planar hypothesis: set `cylindrical_hypothesis = -1`, skip.
    - Search fi's neighbors for a seed partner ni: an adjacent face that is also unassigned (`cylindrical_hypothesis == -2`), also has a single-face planar hypothesis, and has a sufficiently different normal (their cross product magnitude exceeds a minimum threshold, e.g. 0.01, to avoid near-parallel normals that would produce an unreliable axis estimate).
    - If no valid seed partner found: set `cylindrical_hypothesis = -1`, skip. (This face will be considered for spherical or other hypotheses in later stages.)
    - Estimate initial cylinder parameters from the seed pair (fi, ni) — see Cylinder Fitting below.
    - Verify seed validity: check that all vertices of fi and ni are within `--vertex-tolerance` of the estimated cylinder surface. If not, try the next neighbor. If no neighbor produces a valid seed, set `cylindrical_hypothesis = -1` and skip.
```

Additionally, the dihedral angle between fi and ni must not exceed `--angular-tolerance` (default 17.5°) — this prevents seeding from pairs of faces that meet at too sharp an angle (e.g., adjacent faces of a cube at 90°).",

Now find and update the BFS expansion section for cylindrical:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 238-248 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L238-L249">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
    - If ni is already assigned a cylindrical hypothesis, skip.
    - If ni belongs to a multi-face planar hypothesis, skip.
    - **Vertex distance check**: for each vertex of ni, compute `| ||v - axis_closest|| - radius |`. If all are within `--vertex-tolerance`, accept directly. If any exceeds `2 * vertex_tolerance`, skip immediately (re-fitting cannot help, same reasoning as for planar hypotheses). If between 1x and 2x, proceed to re-fit attempt.
    - **Convexity check**: compute the vector from the axis to ni's centroid. Check that ni's face normal agrees with the hypothesis convexity (dot product with radial vector has the expected sign). Reject if inconsistent — this prevents merging the inner and outer surfaces of a thin-walled cylinder.
    - **Re-fit attempt**: if any vertex exceeds tolerance but none exceeds 2x, re-fit the cylinder from all current faces plus ni (see Cylinder Fitting below). Check that **all** vertices (existing and new) are within tolerance of the re-fitted cylinder. If so, accept the re-fit; otherwise skip ni.
    - If accepted: assign ni to the hypothesis, add its vertices to the vertex set, push ni onto the BFS queue.
- After BFS completes: final re-fit from all accumulated faces and vertices. Compute error metrics.
- **Centroid validation**: After BFS completes and error metrics are computed, validate that **all** face centroids lie within `--surface-tolerance` of the fitted cylinder surface. This catches spurious fits (e.g. two perpendicular planar faces that happen to fit a cylinder algebraically but whose centroids are far from any actual cylindrical surface). If validation fails, undo assignments and discard the hypothesis.
- **Minimum face count**: Require at least 3 faces in a cylindrical hypothesis. Any real cylindrical surface from a CAD tessellation will produce at least 3 facets around its circumference. This eliminates spurious 2-face cylinder fits that arise from locally adjacent faces on non-cylindrical curved surfaces (tori, cones, spheres). Combined with centroid validation, this significantly reduces false positives.

**Cylinder fitting** (used for seeding and re-fitting):
```

Searched for text `` cross product magnitude exceeds `MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD` `` (`**/DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md`), 1 result
- [DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md:291](../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L291)

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 289-295 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L289-L296">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
**Seeding:**
- For each face fi where `spherical_hypothesis == UNDEDUCED` (-2):
    - Search fi's neighbors for a seed partner ni: an adjacent face that is also unassigned (`spherical_hypothesis == UNDEDUCED`), and has a sufficiently different normal (cross product magnitude exceeds `MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD` = 0.01).
    - If no valid seed partner found: set `spherical_hypothesis = NO_HYPOTHESIS`, skip.
    - Estimate initial sphere parameters from the seed pair (fi, ni) using least-squares sphere fitting (see Sphere Fitting below). The combined vertices of two non-coplanar faces (at least 6 for two triangles, 8 for two quads) provide enough constraints for the 4-DOF sphere fit.
    - Verify seed validity: check that all vertices of fi and ni are within `--vertex-tolerance` of the estimated sphere surface.
    - **Radius sanity check**: reject the seed if the fitted radius exceeds `max_sphere_radius` (see below). This prevents degenerate fits where a very large sphere approximates locally flat chamfer/bevel faces.
```
</details>

```
    - If ni already has a spherical hypothesis, skip.

    - **Re-fit attempt**: re-fit sphere from all current faces plus ni. Check all vertices within tolerance and radius within `max_sphere_radius`. Accept or reject.

- After BFS completes: final re-fit, compute error metrics.
- **Centroid validation**: all face centroids must be within `--surface-tolerance` of the fitted sphere. Discard hypothesis if validation fails.
```

```
- Simple per-face priority rule: multi-face planar > spherical > cylindrical > single-face planar.
```

Now also add an entry about the angular tolerance algorithm to the relevant cylindrical/spherical learnings sections:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 130-140 of <a href="../LEARNINGS.md#L130-L141">LEARNINGS.md</a></summary>

```
- Sphere poles are an exception: triangles in the polar fan pattern don't have coplanar neighbors and correctly remain as triangles.

### Stage 2.2 cylindrical hypothesis algorithm
- Two-step fitting: (1) axis direction from smallest eigenvector of area-weighted normal covariance matrix M = Σ wᵢ nᵢ nᵢᵀ, (2) axis position and radius via 2D algebraic circle fit after projecting vertices perpendicular to axis.
- 3×3 symmetric eigenvalue computation uses depressed cubic (trigonometric solution with cos(θ/3)), plus null-space eigenvector via cross products of (M - λI) rows.
- BFS seeds from pairs of adjacent single-face planar hypothesis faces with cross product magnitude > 0.01 (MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD).
- Centroid validation after BFS: each face centroid must be within surface_tolerance of the fitted cylinder. This rejects spurious fits from perpendicular planar faces that algebraically fit a cylinder.
- Minimum 3-face requirement: cylinder hypotheses must contain at least 3 faces. Any real cylindrical surface from a CAD tessellation will have at least 3 facets around its circumference. This eliminates spurious 2-face cylinder fits from locally adjacent faces on tori, cones, and spheres (e.g., cone went from 29 to 0 spurious fits, pipe_elbow from 252 to 51).

### ccad box() coordinate system
- `box(w, h, d)` places the box in the positive octant: (0,0,0) to (w,h,d). It does NOT center at origin.
```

```
- Algebraic least-squares sphere fitting: expand |v-c|²=r² linearly into 4 unknowns (cx, cy, cz, k=r²-|c|²). Normal equations AᵀAx = Aᵀb solved via 4×4 Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting.
- BFS seeds from pairs of adjacent faces with non-parallel normals (cross product > MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD=0.01), analogous to cylindrical seeding.
- Original plan called for 3-face seeds with normals-span-3D eigenvalue check, but this failed on finely tessellated spheres where 3 adjacent faces span only ~6° and fall below the eigenvalue ratio threshold. Pair-based seeding works because the 4-DOF sphere fit from 6+ vertices validates the geometry.
- max_sphere_radius = bounding_box_diagonal * 10 prevents degenerate large-radius fits on chamfer/bevel faces.
- Centroid validation after BFS: each face centroid must be within surface_tolerance of the fitted sphere.
- Minimum 4-face requirement: a sphere needs at least 4 non-coplanar points for a unique fit.
- All faces are candidates (including those with cylindrical hypotheses). This allows equator faces to have both cylinder and sphere hypotheses. Stage 2.6 resolves using per-face priority.
- Torus faces locally fit spheres (e.g., pipe_elbow produces 36 sphere hypotheses). This is expected — stage 2.6 with torus hypothesis support will resolve it.
- Concave spheres (bowls/pockets) have normals pointing toward center — same convexity logic as cylinders.
```

```sh
Implement --angular-tolerance flag for cylindrical and spherical fits

Add --angular-tolerance CLI flag (default: 17.5°) that limits the maximum
dihedral angle between adjacent triangles when building surface hypotheses.
CAD tessellators enforce a maximum angular tolerance between adjacent triangles
on the same surface, so faces meeting at angles exceeding this limit cannot
belong to the same curved surface.

- Seed pair creation: reject seed pairs with dihedral angle > limit
- BFS expansion: reject neighbor faces with dihedral angle > limit
  relative to the current BFS-parent face
```

**Summary of what was accomplished:**

Implemented the  flag (default 17.5°) for cylindrical and spherical surface hypothesis generation. The flag limits the maximum dihedral angle between adjacent mesh triangles that can be considered part of the same curved surface.

**How it works:** The check is applied at two points in both cylindrical and spherical fitting:
1. **Seed pair creation** — reject pairs with dihedral angle > limit
2. **BFS expansion** — reject neighbor faces with dihedral angle > limit relative to the BFS-parent

**Result:** The 1mm manual cube's spurious sphere (r=0.866) and cylinder hypotheses are eliminated because adjacent cube faces meet at 90°, far exceeding the 17.5° default. Real cylinders and spheres (with typical tessellation angles of ~6°) are unaffected.

**Tests:** 5 new unit tests validate the mechanism; all 186 existing tests continue to pass.

Now I have a clear picture of the current implementation. Let me think through this carefully.

**The question**: Is checking the dihedral angle only against the BFS parent () sufficient, or should we check against ALL already-assigned neighbors of the candidate?

**Analysis for cylinders:**

On a cylinder, adjacent faces along the circumferential direction have a nonzero dihedral angle (the surface curves), while faces along the axial direction are coplanar (zero dihedral angle). The BFS explores in all directions. Consider a face being explored from its axial neighbor (dihedral ≈ 0°) — it would always pass the angular tolerance check against that parent, even though its circumferential neighbor (already in the hypothesis) might have a large dihedral angle with it. 

But wait — can this actually happen? For a face to have a large dihedral angle with a circumferential neighbor that's already in the hypothesis, that circumferential neighbor must have been accepted earlier. That neighbor was accepted because *its* BFS parent had a small enough angle with it. So the cumulative angular change is bounded by the number of BFS hops times the per-hop tolerance... but the per-hop check guarantees each hop is ≤ angular_tol. The concern would be if the BFS "approaches" a face from a direction where the angle is small, but the face has extreme curvature relative to other already-assigned neighbors.

Actually, on a regular tessellation of a cylinder, the dihedral angle between circumferential neighbors is constant (say θ), and the dihedral angle between axial neighbors is 0°. If BFS reaches face F from its axial neighbor, the check passes (angle ≈ 0°). But F's circumferential neighbor G might already be in the set with angle θ between F and G. If θ > angular_tol, then BFS via the circumferential route would have rejected F, but BFS via the axial route accepts it. This is a real gap!

However — is this actually a problem in practice? If θ > angular_tol between neighboring faces on the same cylinder, then the cylinder is either very tight-radius or very coarsely tessellated. The angular_tol is meant to match the CAD tessellator's setting. If the tessellator produced the mesh with angular tolerance α, then ALL adjacent faces on the same surface should have dihedral angle ≤ α. So circumferential neighbors also satisfy the constraint. The BFS-parent-only check is sufficient *given the tessellation invariant*.

The concern would only arise if faces from *different* surfaces were being incorrectly grouped. But faces from different surfaces have a geometric boundary between them — the vertex distance check and convexity check already catch those.

Let me think about the cube case specifically. The cube has exactly 6 faces (after quad fusion). Each face's neighbors are the other 4 adjacent faces, all at 90° dihedral angle. BFS starts with a seed pair — both faces must have dihedral ≤ angular_tol. For a cube, 90° > 17.5°, so no seed pair is formed. The check-against-parent is sufficient here because you can't even seed.

**The real question is**: can the BFS-parent-only check cause faces from *different surfaces* to be grouped when a check-against-all-assigned-neighbors would prevent it? 

Consider two perpendicular planes meeting at an edge. Face A is on plane 1, face B is on plane 2, at 90° angle. BFS would never group them via the parent check (90° > 17.5°). But what if there's an intermediate face C between them that's at 45° to both? BFS from A accepts C (45° > 17.5° — actually that would fail too). With a very generous angular tolerance, say 50°, BFS from A could accept C (45° < 50°), then from C accept B (45° < 50°), but A-to-B is 90° which exceeds 50°. Checking against all neighbors would catch this.

But this scenario doesn't arise on real CAD tessellations, because:
1. The tessellator's angular tolerance applies per-edge, not accumulatively
2. Faces on *different* surfaces meet at boundary edges where vertex distance checks also fail

**My conclusion**: The BFS-parent-only check is sufficient for correctly tessellated CAD models, because the angular tolerance invariant applies per-edge from the tessellator. Checking against all assigned neighbors would be more robust as a defense-in-depth measure but wouldn't change behavior on well-formed inputs.

The parent-only check is sufficient. Here's why:

**The CAD tessellation invariant is per-edge, not cumulative.** When a CAD tessellator (Onshape, Fusion 360) generates mesh faces on a single surface with angular tolerance α, it guarantees that *every pair of adjacent faces* on that surface has dihedral angle ≤ α. This means any neighbor of a candidate face that's already in the hypothesis also satisfies the angular tolerance — not just the BFS parent.

**Cylinder case**: Adjacent faces in the circumferential direction have dihedral angle θ (constant for regular tessellation). Adjacent faces in the axial direction have dihedral angle ≈ 0°. If a face is reached via its axial neighbor (angle ≈ 0°), you might worry that its circumferential neighbor already in the set has angle θ that wasn't checked. But since the tessellator guarantees θ ≤ α for all same-surface adjacencies, this is ) sufficient, or should we check against ALL already-assigned neighbors of the candidate?

**Analysis for cylinders:**

On a cylinder, adjacent faces along the circumferential direction have a nonzero dihedral angle (the surface curves), while faces along the axial direction are coplanar (zero dihedral angle). The BFS explores in all directions. Consider a face being explored from its axial neighbor (dihedral ≈ 0°) — it would always pass the angular tolerance check against that parent, even though its circumferential neighbor (already in the hypothesis) might have a large dihedral angle with it. 

But wait — can this actually happen? For a face to have a large dihedral angle with a circumferential neighbor that's already in the hypothesis, that circumferential neighbor must have been accepted earlier. That neighbor was accepted because *its* BFS parent had a small enough angle with it. So the cumulative angular change is bounded by the number of BFS hops times the per-hop tolerance... but the per-hop check guarantees each hop is ≤ angular_tol. The concern would be if the BFS "approaches" a face from a direction where the angle is small, but the face has extreme curvature relative to other already-assigned neighbors.

Actually, on a regular tessellation of a cylinder, the dihedral angle between circumferential neighbors is constant (say θ), and the dihedral angle between axial neighbors is 0°. If BFS reaches face F from its axial neighbor, the check passes (angle ≈ 0°). But F's circumferential neighbor G might already be in the set with angle θ between F and G. If θ > angular_tol, then BFS via the circumferential route would have rejected F, but BFS via the axial route accepts it. This is a real gap!

However — is this actually a problem in practice? If θ > angular_tol between neighboring faces on the same cylinder, then the cylinder is either very tight-radius or very coarsely tessellated. The angular_tol is meant to match the CAD tessellator's setting. If the tessellator produced the mesh with angular tolerance α, then ALL adjacent faces on the same surface should have dihedral angle ≤ α. So circumferential neighbors also satisfy the constraint. The BFS-parent-only check is sufficient *given the tessellation invariant*.

The concern would only arise if faces from *different* surfaces were being incorrectly grouped. But faces from different surfaces have a geometric boundary between them — the vertex distance check and convexity check already catch those.

Let me think about the cube case specifically. The cube has exactly 6 faces (after quad fusion). Each face's neighbors are the other 4 adjacent faces, all at 90° dihedral angle. BFS starts with a seed pair — both faces must have dihedral ≤ angular_tol. For a cube, 90° > 17.5°, so no seed pair is formed. The check-against-parent is sufficient here because you can't even seed.

**The real question is**: can the BFS-parent-only check cause faces from *different surfaces* to be grouped when a check-against-all-assigned-neighbors would prevent it? 

Consider two perpendicular planes meeting at an edge. Face A is on plane 1, face B is on plane 2, at 90° angle. BFS would never group them via the parent check (90° > 17.5°). But what if there's an intermediate face C between them that's at 45° to both? BFS from A accepts C (45° > 17.5° — actually that would fail too). With a very generous angular tolerance, say 50°, BFS from A could accept C (45° < 50°), then from C accept B (45° < 50°), but A-to-B is 90° which exceeds 50°. Checking against all neighbors would catch this.

But this scenario doesn't arise on real CAD tessellations, because:
1. The tessellator's angular tolerance applies per-edge, not accumulatively
2. Faces on *different* surfaces meet at boundary edges where vertex distance checks also fail

**My conclusion**: The BFS-parent-only check is sufficient for correctly tessellated CAD models, because the angular tolerance invariant applies per-edge from the tessellator. Checking against all assigned neighbors would be more robust as a defense-in-depth measure but wouldn't change behavior on well-formed inputs.

The parent-only check is sufficient. Here's why:

**The CAD tessellation invariant is per-edge, not cumulative.** When a CAD tessellator (Onshape, Fusion 360) generates mesh faces on a single surface with angular tolerance α, it guarantees that *every pair of adjacent faces* on that surface has dihedral angle ≤ α. This means any neighbor of a candidate face that's already in the hypothesis also satisfies the angular tolerance — not just the BFS parent.

**Cylinder case**: Adjacent faces in the circumferential direction have dihedral angle θ (constant for regular tessellation). Adjacent faces in the axial direction have dihedral angle ≈ 0°. If a face is reached via its axial neighbor (angle ≈ 0°), you might worry that its circumferential neighbor already in the set has angle θ that wasn't checked. But since the tessellator guarantees θ ≤ α for all same-surface adjacencies, this is fine — θ would pass the check against any neighbor.

**Sphere case**: Same reasoning. On a sphere, dihedral angles between adjacent faces vary somewhat (larger near poles, smaller at equator on typical tessellations), but the tessellator guarantees all are ≤ α.

**The only scenario where parent-only fails but all-neighbors catches a problem**: An intermediate face at angle α/2+ε to two other faces that are at angle α+2ε to each other. The BFS accepts via the intermediate face, but a direct check against the far face would reject. This CAN'T happen on a single CAD surface (tessellator invariant), and on different surfaces the vertex-distance and convexity checks prevent grouping.

**Verdict**: No code change needed. The parent-only check is correct because  is designed to match the tessellator's per-edge guarantee, and the tessellator applies that guarantee uniformly to all adjacent face pairs on the same surface. Checking against all assigned neighbors would add O(degree) work per BFS step without changing the outcome on any realistic input.

Good challenge. Let me reconsider carefully.

**The key insight in your question**: my argument relied on the tessellator enforcing angular tolerance per-edge on a *smooth* surface. A NURBS surface with a C0 crease (normal discontinuity) would be tessellated as a single surface, but adjacent mesh faces across the crease could have a dihedral angle far exceeding the angular tolerance setting.

**But here's why it still doesn't matter for the parent-only check:**

Mesh face normals are computed from vertex positions (Newell's method in stage 1.2), NOT from the underlying NURBS parameterization. A "crease" in the NURBS only manifests in the mesh if the vertex positions themselves create a geometric fold. So there are two cases:

1. **Vertices on both sides of the crease lie on the same cylinder/sphere**: Then the mesh face normals are determined by those vertex positions and will be consistent with the cylinder/sphere geometry. There's no dihedral discontinuity in the mesh — the NURBS crease is invisible at the mesh level. Parent-only check works fine.

2. **The crease creates a geometric fold in the vertex positions**: Then vertices on one side deviate from the fitted cylinder/sphere, and the vertex distance check rejects them. The angular tolerance check isn't even reached.

**The scenario where parent-only could theoretically fail** is: face X has neighbors A (in hypothesis, angle 5°) and C (in hypothesis, angle 25° > tolerance). BFS from A accepts X via the small angle. But this requires both X and C to have vertices on the cylinder — meaning the mesh normals of X and C are both consistent with the cylinder geometry. If they're both geometrically on the cylinder, the 25° angle between X and C corresponds to real curvature of the cylinder, which is fine — they're on the same surface.

**Bottom line**: The parent-only check is sufficient because the angular tolerance is defending against faces that are on *different* geometric surfaces being grouped together (like cube faces). For faces whose vertices all lie on the same cylinder/sphere, a large dihedral angle between them just means the tessellation is coarse in that region — but the grouping is geometrically correct. Checking against all neighbors would reject correct groupings on coarsely-tessellated cylinders approached from the axial direction.

That said — if you'd prefer defense-in-depth, checking all assigned neighbors is cheap (O(degree) ≈ O(3-4) per candidate). Want me to add it?

```
    - **Vertex distance check**: for each vertex of ni, compute `| ||v - center|| - radius |`. If all within `--vertex-tolerance`, accept directly. If any exceeds `2 * vertex_tolerance`, skip. If between 1x and 2x, re-fit.
    - **Convexity check**: verify ni's normal agrees with the hypothesis convexity (dot product of normal with radial vector has the expected sign).
    - **Angular tolerance check**: compute the dihedral angle between ni and the current BFS-parent face. If it exceeds `--angular-tolerance`, reject.
    - **Re-fit attempt**: re-fit sphere from all current faces plus ni. Check all vertices within tolerance and radius within `max_sphere_radius`. Accept or reject.
    - If accepted: assign ni, add vertices, push onto BFS queue.
```
</details>

**Terminal:**
```sh
git commit -F - <<'EOF'
Angular tolerance: check all assigned neighbors, not just BFS parent

Change the angular tolerance check in both cylindrical and spherical BFS
expansion to verify the candidate face against ALL of its mesh neighbors
that are already assigned to the current hypothesis, rather than only the
BFS parent face.

This provides defense-in-depth against creased NURBS surfaces where BFS
might approach a face from a low-angle direction (e.g. axially on a
cylinder) while a high-angle assigned neighbor exists on a different axis
(e.g. circumferentially). On well-formed CAD tessellations of smooth
analytic surfaces this makes no difference (the tessellator's per-edge
angular tolerance guarantee means all same-surface adjacencies satisfy
the constraint), but it guards against edge cases with C0-discontinuous
surfaces.

Updated DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md to reflect the change for both stages 2.2
and 2.3.
```
